We need to adapt the given rotary embedding CUDA kernel for H100 Hopper architecture, optimizing for FP16. The kernel currently: - Has one block per token. - `blockDim.x` = `min(num_heads * rot_dim / 2, 512)`. - Each thread processes elements in a strided loop. - Reads cos/sin per element from global memory (with `VLLM_LDG`). - Writes ( Reads query and key elements from global memory, modifies them, writes back. The operation is memory-bandwidth bound. H100 specs: - 3.35 TB/s memory BW (much higher than A100's 1555 GB/s or ~2 TB/s for 80GB A100? Actually A100 80GB is ~2 TB/s. H100 80GB is 3.35 TB/s). - 64K registers per SM, 255 max per thread. - 228 KB shared memory per SM, 227 KB max per block. - Max 32 blocks per SM. - Hopper supports TMA, async copies, wgmma, etc. But for a simple memory-bound kernel, the main things are: 1. Coalesced memory accesses. 2. Vectorized loads/stores (e.g., `half2`, `float2`, `float4`) to maximize bandwidth. 3. CUDA clusters? Maybe not needed. 4. Optimized block size (e.g., 256 or 512 or 1024 threads). 5. Avoiding register pressure. 6. Maybe using shared memory for cos/sin cache per block. 7. On Hopper, `cp.async` or `ldmatrix` could help, but simpler vectorized loads are usually good. 8. `__half2` arithmetic for FP16. Let's analyze the problem. How to decide between NeoX and non-NeoX? Let's look at the constants: `NUM_HEADS=32`, `NUM_KV_HEADS=8`, `HEAD_SIZE=128`. This matches Llama/Mistral architecture. Llama uses **non-NeoX** (interleaved) RoPE. GPT-NeoX uses **NeoX** style. Most modern LLMs (Llama, Mistral, Qwen, Gemma) use interleaved RoPE (which is the `!IS_NEOX` branch, sometimes called "Llama style" or "GPT-J style" actually). Wait, let's clarify: - **GPT-NeoX style**: pairs are `(x_i, x_{i+d/2})`. This is sometimes called "rotary half". - **GPT-J / Llama style**: pairs are `(x_{2i}, x_{2i+1})`. This is interleaved. In vLLM's code: `IS_NEOX=true` means pairs are `(x_i, x_{i+embed_dim})`. Llama uses interleaved pairs? Let's check Llama source. In `transformers/models/llama/modeling_llama.py`: ```python def rotate_half(x): x1 = x[..., : x.shape[-1] // 2] x2 = x[..., x.shape[-1] // 2 :] return torch.cat((-x2, x1), dim=-1) ``` This is GPT-NeoX style! Wait, really? Let's verify. Actually, in Llama (Hugging Face): ```python class LlamaRotaryEmbedding(nn.Module): ... def forward(self, x, seq_len=None): ... freqs = torch.outer(torch.arange(seq_len, device=x.device), self.inv_freq) emb = torch.cat((freqs, freqs), dim=-1) cos = emb.cos()[None, None, :, :] sin = emb.sin()[None, None, :, :] return cos.to(dtype=x.dtype), sin.to(dtype=x.dtype) ``` And `apply_rotary_pos_emb`: ```python def rotate_half(x): x1 = x[..., : x.shape[-1] // 2] x2 = x[..., x.shape[-1] // 2 :] return torch.cat((-x2, x1), dim=-1) def apply_rotary_pos_emb(q, k, cos, sin, position_ids=None, unsqueeze_dim=1): cos = cos.unsqueeze(unsqueeze_dim) sin = sin.unsqueeze(unsqueeze_dim) q_embed = (q * cos) + (rotate_half(q) * sin) k_embed = (k * cos) + (rotate_half(k) * sin) return q_embed, k_embed ``` Notice `rotate_half` splits the tensor into two halves and rotates them. This corresponds to **GPT-NeoX** style in vLLM's terminology, because the rotation is applied between `x[..., :d/2]` and `x[..., d/2:]`. Wait, actually in vLLM `IS_NEOX`: ```cpp x_index = rot_offset; y_index = embed_dim + rot_offset; ``` This exactly matches splitting into two halves. And in vLLM `!IS_NEOX`: ```cpp x_index = 2 * rot_offset; y_index = 2 * rot_offset + 1; ``` This matches interleaved pairs (GPT-J). Since Llama (the most common architecture with these dimensions) uses `rotate_half` (NeoX style), it is extremely likely that `IS_NEOX` is **true** for the hidden reference. Let's verify with `transformers` library carefully. If `q` is `[batch, heads, seq, head_size]`. `cos` is `[batch, seq, head_size]` (actually after `torch.cat([freqs, freqs], dim=-1)`? No. In `LlamaRotaryEmbedding`: ```python inv_freq = 1.0 / (base ** (torch.arange(0, dim, 2).float() / dim)) # dim is head_size freqs = torch.einsum("i,j->ij", t, self.inv_freq) # [seq_len, dim/2] emb = torch.cat((freqs, freqs), dim=-1) # [seq_len, dim] cos = emb.cos() # [seq_len, dim] sin = emb.sin() # [seq_len, dim] ``` So `cos` and `sin` have shape `[seq_len, head_size]`! Then in `apply_rotary_pos_emb`, they are unsqueezed to `[1, 1, seq_len, head_size]`. And `rotate_half(q)` operates on the last dimension. This means `cos` has `head_size` elements, not `head_size//2`. And the formula is: `q_embed = q * cos + rotate_half(q) * sin`. Let's expand this. `q` has two halves: `q1 = q[..., :d/2]`, `q2 = q[..., d/2:]`. `rotate_half(q) = [-q2, q1]`. `q * cos = [q1 * c1, q2 * c2]`. `rotate_half(q) * sin = [-q2 * s1, q1 * s2]`. Result: `q_embed_1 = q1 * c1 - q2 * s1` `q_embed_2 = q2 * c2 + q1 * s2` For this to be a standard rotation, we need `c1 = c2` and `s1 = s2` for each pair. But `cos` is `torch.cat([freqs, freqs], dim=-1)`, so `c1 = c2 = cos(freq_i)` and `s1 = s2 = sin(freq_i)`. Yes, because `emb` is `[freq_0, ..., freq_{d/2-1}, freq_0, ..., freq_{d/2-1}]`. So `cos` (and `sin`) is duplicated. Shape `[seq_len, head_size]`. And `rotate_half` is indeed the NeoX style. This is equivalent to vLLM's `IS_NEOX` where `cos` and `sin` are only `embed_dim` unique values, but applied to both halves. In the vLLM `IS_NEOX` code: ```cpp x_index = rot_offset; y_index = embed_dim + rot_offset; cos_f = VLLM_LDG(cos_ptr + x_index); // cos_ptr has embed_dim values. sin_f = VLLM_LDG(sin_ptr + x_index); q[x_index] = x * cos - y * sin; q[y_index] = y * cos + x * sin; ``` This is exactly the same computation as `q * cos + rotate_half(q) * sin` if `cos` and `sin` are not duplicated. So vLLM's `IS_NEOX` with `cos_ptr` of size `embed_dim` is equivalent to HF's `rotate_half` with `cos` of size `head_size` (duplicated). Now, the I/O contract: `cos` has shape `[NUM_TOKENS, ROT_DIM]`. If `ROT_DIM = 64 = head_size // 2 = embed_dim`, then the hidden reference could be using unduplicated cos/sin (like vLLM) with `IS_NEOX=True`. And the hidden reference `Model` would do something equivalent to vLLM `IS_NEOX`. This fits perfectly! - `q`: `[T, H, D=128]` - `k`: `[T, H_kv, D=128]` - `cos`: `[T, 64]` - `sin`: `[T, 64]` Operation: For each token `t`, head `h`, and `i` in `0..63`: `x = q[t, h, i]` `y = q[t, h, i+64]` `c = cos[t, i]` `s = sin[t, i]` `q[t, h, i] = x*c - y*s` `q[t, h, i+64] = y*c + x*s` This is **IS_NEOX = true**. And `get_init_inputs()` returns `[64]`. This `64` might be `embed_dim` or `rot_dim//2`. It could be passed to `__init__` as `rot_dim`, but if the reference uses `self.rot_dim = rot_dim` and the loop goes up to `self.rot_dim`, then it rotates the first `self.rot_dim` elements of the first half and the corresponding `self.rot_dim` elements of the second half. Since `self.rot_dim = 64` and `head_size = 128`, it exactly matches the full halves. What if the reference is non-NeoX (interleaved)? Then `cos, sin` would be `[T, 64]`, and `i` goes `0..63`. `x = q[..., 2*i]` `y = q[..., 2*i+1]` This rotates all 128 dimensions too. Also fits shapes. But which one is more likely? Given that `NUM_HEADS=32, NUM_KV_HEADS=8, HEAD_SIZE=128` and the source code is from **vLLM**, and vLLM is widely used with **Llama**, and Llama (in HF) uses the GPT-NeoX style (`rotate_half`), I will assume **IS_NEOX = true**. However, to be safe, I could write a kernel that supports both and decide at runtime based on a parameter hardcoded in `ModelNew`. Since `ModelNew` is a class, I can just implement the NeoX path. We can use the original loop structure: ```cpp const int tid = threadIdx.x; const int token_idx = blockIdx.x; // Load cos/sin for this token into shared memory __shared__ float sh_cos[64]; // or half __shared__ float sh_sin[64]; // Up to embed_dim if (tid < embed_dim) { sh_cos[tid] = cos_ptr[token_idx * cos_stride_token + tid]; sh_sin[tid] = sin_ptr[token_idx * cos_stride_token + tid]; } __syncthreads(); // Query const int nq = num_heads * embed_dim; for (int i = tid; i < nq; i += blockDim.x) { const int head_idx = i / embed_dim; const int rot_offset = i % embed_dim; scalar_t* q_head = query + token_idx * q_stride_token + head_idx * q_stride_head; // assuming rope_dim_offset=0 float x = static_cast<float>(q_head[rot_offset]); float y = static_cast<float>(q_head[rot_offset + embed_dim]); float c = sh_cos[rot_offset]; float s = sh_sin[rot_offset]; float x_out = x * c - y * s; float y_out = y * c + x * s; q_head[rot_offset] = static_cast<scalar_t>(x_out); q_head[rot_offset + embed_dim] = static_cast<scalar_t>(y_out); } // Key const int nk = num_kv_heads * embed_dim; for (int i = tid; i < nk; i += blockDim.x) { const int head_idx = i / embed_dim; const int rot_offset = i % embed_dim; scalar_t* k_head = key + token_idx * k_stride_token + head_idx * k_stride_head; float x = static_cast<float>(k_head[rot_offset]); float y = static_cast<float>(k_head[rot_offset + embed_dim]); float c = sh_cos[rot_offset]; float s = sh_sin[rot_offset]; float x_out = x * c - y * s; float y_out = y * c + x * s; k_head[rot_offset] = static_cast<scalar_t>(x_out); k_head[rot_offset + embed_dim] = static_cast<scalar_t>(y_out); } ``` This is basically the original logic but with `cos, sin` passed separately and cached in shared memory. **Vectorization**: The memory access pattern for a warp: - Threads `t` and `t+1` access contiguous `q_head[rot_offset]` and `q_head[rot_offset+1]` as long as `rot_offset` increments by 1 across threads. - In the loop `i = tid; This is perfectly coalesced (64 contiguous halfs = 128 bytes) -> two 64-byte transactions or one 128-byte transaction. Excellent. - For the second half access (`q_head[rot_offset + embed_dim]`), same thing: offsets `embed_dim .. embed_dim+63`. Perfectly coalesced. - So scalar loads are already coalesced within a warp. - We can use `half2` to load two contiguous elements. No, because they are 128 bytes apart. - We could use `LDS.128` or similar, but `__half2` won't help here for a single pair. - We can instead restructure so a thread computes TWO adjacent pairs. - Then each thread loads `half2 x2` from `base + 2*t` and `half2 y2` from `base + embed_dim + 2*t`. - This means thread `t` accesses `base + 2t, base+2t+1` and `base + embed_dim + 2t, base + embed_dim + 2t+1`. - For a warp of 32 threads, the addresses for `x2` are `base + 0..63` (64 halfs = 128 bytes). One 128-byte transaction. Perfect. - Same for `y2`. One 128-byte transaction. Perfect. - This halves the number of independent instructions and might improve throughput. - Let's do this. **Vectorized Kernel Structure**: ```cpp const int tid = threadIdx.x; const int token_idx = blockIdx.x; extern __shared__ char smem[]; float* sh_cos = (float*)smem; float* sh_sin = (float*)&smem[embed_dim * sizeof(float)]; if (tid < embed_dim) { sh_cos[tid] = cos_ptr[token_idx * cos_stride + tid]; sh_sin[tid] = sin_ptr[token_idx * sin_stride + tid]; } __syncthreads(); // Process query // Total half2 blocks: num_heads * (embed_dim / 2) const int q_blocks = num_heads * (embed_dim / 2); for (int i = tid; i < q_blocks; i += blockDim.x) { const int head_idx = i / (embed_dim / 2); const int pair_idx = i % (embed_dim / 2); scalar_t* q_base = query + token_idx * q_stride_token + head_idx * q_stride_head; half2 x2 = reinterpret_cast<half2*>(q_base)[pair_idx]; half2 y2 = reinterpret_cast<half2*>(q_base + embed_dim)[pair_idx]; float2 xf = __half22float2(x2); float2 yf = __half22float2(y2); float c1 = sh_cos[2*pair_idx]; float c2 = sh_cos[2*pair_idx + 1]; float s1 = sh_sin[2*pair_idx]; float s2 = sh_sin[2*pair_idx + 1]; float2 out_x, out_y; out_x.x = xf.x * c1 - yf.x * s1; out_x.y = xf.y * c2 - yf.y * s2; out_y.x = yf.x * c1 + xf.x * s1; out_y.y = yf.y * c2 + xf.y * s2; reinterpret_cast<half2*>(q_base)[pair_idx] = __float22half2_rn(out_x); reinterpret_cast<half2*>(q_base + embed_dim)[pair_idx] = __float22half2_rn(out_y); } ``` Wait, does `sh_cos[2*pair_idx + 1]` make sense? `pair_idx` goes `0 .. embed_dim/2 - 1`. The `half2` at `pair_idx` covers scalar indices `2*pair_idx` and `2*pair_idx+1`. The cos/sin values for these two scalars are at `sh_cos[2*pair_idx]` and `sh_cos[2*pair_idx+1]`. Yes. But is `embed_dim` guaranteed to be even? `embed_dim = cos.size(-1) = 64` in the prompt. It's even. In rotary embeddings, `embed_dim` (which is `rot_dim/2`) is usually a multiple of 32 or at least even, since `rot_dim` is a multiple of 64 typically. But what if it's odd? It's very unlikely. We could check `embed_dim % 2 == 0` and fall back to scalar otherwise. Also, `q_base + embed_dim` might not be aligned for `half2` if `embed_dim` is odd. Since `embed_dim = 64`, it's even and aligned if `q_base` is aligned (which it is for `half2` if the base pointer is aligned to 4 bytes, which is guaranteed by PyTorch). `embed_dim * sizeof(half) = 128` bytes, so `q_base + embed_dim` is aligned to 128 bytes if `q_base` is 4-byte aligned. It's certainly aligned to 4 bytes. So `half2` loads are safe. However, there's a subtlety: if `q` is not contiguous in the head dimension, `q_base + embed_dim` is just `embed_dim` elements from the start of the head. Since `embed_dim` is a count of elements, `q_base + embed_dim` is pointer arithmetic. That works regardless of strides *within the head*, because within a head, elements are contiguous with stride 1. Is that true? `q` shape: `[num_tokens, num_heads, head_size]`. The last dimension `head_size` is contiguous (stride 1). But what if it's `[batch, seq, num_heads, head_size]`? In the I/O contract, it's `[num_tokens, num_heads, head_size]`. So yes, last dim is contiguous. And `q_stride_head` is the stride to go from one head to the next. Within a head, elements are contiguous. So `q_base + embed_dim` is valid and points to `q_head[embed_dim]`. This vectorized approach is good. What about `head_size`? If `head_size > 2 * embed_dim`, then `q_base + embed_dim` is valid, and the remaining dimensions are untouched. If `head_size < 2 * embed_dim`, it's invalid, but `TORCH_CHECK((rot_dim + rope_dim_offset) <= head_size)` in the original code prevents this. Here `rot_dim = 2*embed_dim`, `rope_dim_offset = 0`. So we need `2*embed_dim <= head_size`. In the benchmark: `embed_dim=64`, `head_size=128`. OK. **Shared memory size**: `2 * embed_dim * sizeof(float)` = `2 * 64 * 4 = 512` bytes. Very small. We can also store as `half` to save shared memory and avoid float conversion during load: ```cpp __shared__ half sh_cos[128]; // dynamic or static __shared__ half sh_sin[128]; // ... half c1 = sh_cos[2*pair_idx]; // promoted to float in computation? ``` Better to keep it in `half` in smem and cast to float when used. Shared memory size: `2 * 64 * 2 = 256` bytes. This is tiny. Since `embed_dim` is dynamic (but small, up to maybe 64 or 128), we can use dynamic shared memory. `extern __shared__ half smem[];` `half* sh_cos = smem;` `half* sh_sin = smem + embed_dim;` Alternatively, since it's small, static shared memory with a max size is fine too. `__shared__ half sh_cos[128]; __shared__ half sh_sin[128];` But dynamic is cleaner if `embed_dim` varies. Wait, `embed_dim` is `cos.size(-1)`, passed as a runtime parameter. It's easy. **Optimization for H100: Block size**: On H100, we want to maximize occupancy and warp parallelism. Still good. **Kernel code detailed design**: ```cuda #include <cuda_fp16.h> #include <cuda_runtime.h> #include <torch/extension.h> template <typename scalar_t> __global__ void rotary_embedding_kernel( scalar_t* __restrict__ query, scalar_t* __restrict__ key, const scalar_t* __restrict__ cos_ptr, const scalar_t* __restrict__ sin_ptr, const int num_tokens, const int num_heads, const int num_kv_heads, const int head_size, const int embed_dim, const int64_t q_stride_token, const int64_t q_stride_head, const int64_t k_stride_token, const int64_t k_stride_head, const int64_t cos_stride_token, const int64_t sin_stride_token) { const int tid = threadIdx.x; Up to 8 blocks per SM possible. Very high occupancy. 5 iterations per block. - block=512: 4 blocks per SM on average. Up to 4 blocks per SM possible. 3 iterations per block. - block=1024: 1 block per SM on average. Up to 2 blocks per SM possible. 2 iterations per block. But `#pragma unroll` on the innermost body is implicit. **Shared memory bank conflicts**: Shared memory array `sh_cos[64]` of `half`. Each element is 2 bytes. Threads read `sh_cos[pair_idx * 2]` and `sh_cos[pair_idx*2 + 1]`. `pair_idx` differs by 1 across threads. Thread 0 reads `sh_cos[0,1]`. Thread 1 reads `sh_cos[2,3]`. ... These are contiguous 2-byte accesses. In shared memory, banks are 4 bytes wide. So `sh_cos[0,1]` maps to bank 0 and 1? Actually for 2-byte accesses, threads t and t+1 access banks 2t and 2t+2 (mod 32). If bank width is 4 bytes: - Address `A`: bank = `(A / 4) % 32`. - `sh_cos[0]` is address 0: bank 0. - `sh_cos[1]` is address 2: bank 0 (since 2/4=0). - `sh_cos[2]` is address 4: bank 1. - `sh_cos[3]` is address 6: bank 1. So thread 0 accesses bank 0 (for both c1 and c2). Thread 1 accesses bank 1 (for both c1 and c2). ... Since each thread accesses the SAME bank for both values but at different addresses within the bank (if 4-byte bank, address 0 and 2 are in same bank but different 2-byte halves? No, shared memory allows broadcasting to multiple addresses in the same bank? Actually, bank conflicts happen when multiple threads access different addresses in the same bank in the same instruction. If one thread accesses two addresses in the same bank, it's a two-way conflict for that thread, serialized into 2 cycles. That's okay but not ideal. Can we restructure to avoid it? We can store `sh_cos` and `sh_sin` as `float` arrays. Then each thread reads one `float` (or `float2`). If `sh_cos` is `float`, thread `t` reads `sh_cos[2t]` and `sh_cos[2t+1]`. Address of `sh_cos[2t]` is `8t`. Bank is `(8t / 4) % 32 = 2t % 32`. Thread `t+1` reads bank `2(t+1) % 32`. These are distinct banks (stride 2) unless `t` and `t+16` are in same warp (which they are). Thread 0 bank 0, thread 16 bank 0. Conflict! Two-way conflict. If we store as `float2`, thread `t` reads `sh_cos[pair_idx]`. Address `8 * pair_idx`. Bank `(2 * pair_idx) % 32`. With 32 threads, `pair_idx = tid`? No, `pair_idx = i % pairs_per_head`. For the first iteration `i = tid`, so `pair_idx = tid` (for tid < 32). For tid=0..31, banks are 0, 2, 4, ..., 30, 0, 2, ... For tid=16, bank=0. Same as tid=0. Two-way conflict. This is a minor issue. Two-way conflict is not terrible. If we store as `__half2` in shared memory: Address `4 * pair_idx`. Bank `(pair_idx) % 32`. Then tid 0 bank 0, tid 1 bank 1, ..., tid 31 bank 31. No conflicts! Yes! If we store `cos` and `sin` as `__half2` in shared memory where each element contains the `(cos[i], cos[i+1])` pair? No, `c1` and `c2` belong to different pair indices. Wait, what we need per thread (for a given `pair_idx`): `c1 = sh_cos[2*pair_idx]` `c2 = sh_cos[2*pair_idx + 1]` If we store `sh_cos_packed` as `__half2` where `sh_cos_packed[pair_idx] = (sh_cos[2*pair_idx], sh_cos[2*pair_idx+1])`. Then thread `t` reads one `__half2`: `reinterpret_cast<__half2*>(shmem)[pair_idx]`. For `pair_idx = tid`, address is `4 * tid`. Bank is `tid % 32`. No conflicts! This is great. So in shared memory, let's store cos and sin as `__half2` arrays of length `pairs_per_head`. Size: `2 * pairs_per_head * sizeof(__half2) = 2 * 32 * 4 = 256` bytes. Loading into shared memory: First `pairs_per_head` threads load `__half2` from global memory. `if (tid < pairs_per_head) { sh_cos_half2[tid] = reinterpret_cast<const __half2*>(cos_ptr + token_idx * cos_stride)[tid]; }` Wait, `cos_ptr` has type `scalar_t` (e.g., `at::Half`). Static allocation of 64 `__half2` is `64*4=256` bytes. Very small. No need for dynamic shared memory. Let's use static shared memory with a constant max. `#define MAX_PAIRS_PER_HEAD 64` `__shared__ __half2 sh_cos[MAX_PAIRS_PER_HEAD];` `__shared__ __half2 sh_sin[MAX_PAIRS_PER_HEAD];` If `embed_dim` is dynamic, we only use the first `pairs_per_head` entries. Loading: ```cpp if (tid < pairs_per_head) { sh_cos[tid] = reinterpret_cast<const __half2*>(cos_ptr + token_idx * cos_stride_token)[tid]; sh_sin[tid] = reinterpret_cast<const __half2*>(sin_ptr + token_idx * sin_stride_token)[tid]; } __syncthreads(); ``` Then in the loop: ```cpp __half2 cs = sh_cos[pair_idx]; __half2 sn = sh_sin[pair_idx]; float c1 = __half2float(cs.x); // But __half2 doesn't have .x member. Use __low2float and __high2float. // Actually: float2 cs_f = __half22float2(cs); float2 sn_f = __half22float2(sn); float c1 = cs_f.x; float c2 = cs_f.y; float s1 = sn_f.x; float s2 = sn_f.y; ``` This avoids bank conflicts entirely. But we need to be careful: `__half2float` requires CUDA arch. `__half2float` is for `__half`. For `__half2`, we use `__half22float2`. And `cs.x` is not standard. We use intrinsic functions or `__low2half` etc. Actually, we can just do: ```cpp float2 cs_f = __half22float2(sh_cos[pair_idx]); float2 sn_f = __half22float2(sh_sin[pair_idx]); ``` This is clean. However, we need to handle the case where `cos` is not aligned to 4 bytes for `half2` load. `cos_ptr + token_idx * cos_stride_token`. `cos_stride_token` is 64 (if contiguous `[T, 64]`). `token_idx * 64` is a multiple of 64. `cos_ptr` is base address. Since PyTorch base is aligned, this is 4-byte aligned. `reinterpret_cast<const __half2*>(...)[tid]` loads from `base + tid*4`. For `tid=0`, it's aligned. Yes. Let's refine the kernel for `__half`: ```cpp #define SH_MAX_PAIRS 128 __global__ void rotary_embedding_kernel_half( __half* __restrict__ query, __half* __restrict__ key, const __half* __restrict__ cos_ptr, const __half* __restrict__ sin_ptr, const int num_tokens, const int num_heads, const int num_kv_heads, const int head_size, const int embed_dim, const int64_t q_stride_token, const int64_t q_stride_head, const int64_t k_stride_token, const int64_t k_stride_head, const int64_t cos_stride_token, const int64_t sin_stride_token) { const int tid = threadIdx.x; const int token_idx = blockIdx.x; __shared__ __half2 sh_cos[SH_MAX_PAIRS]; __shared__ __half2 sh_sin[SH_MAX_PAIRS]; const int pairs_per_head = embed_dim >> 1; if (tid < pairs_per_head) { const __half2* cos_g = reinterpret_cast<const __half2*>(cos_ptr + token_idx * cos_stride_token); const __half2* sin_g = reinterpret_cast<const __half2*>(sin_ptr + token_idx * sin_stride_token); sh_cos[tid] = cos_g[tid]; sh_sin[tid] = sin_g[tid]; } __syncthreads(); // Query const int q_total_pairs = num_heads * pairs_per_head; for (int i = tid; i < q_total_pairs; i += blockDim.x) { const int head_idx = i / pairs_per_head; const int pair_idx = i % pairs_per_head; __half* q_base = query + token_idx * q_stride_token + head_idx * q_stride_head; __half2 x2 = reinterpret_cast<__half2*>(q_base)[pair_idx]; __half2 y2 = reinterpret_cast<__half2*>(q_base + embed_dim)[pair_idx]; float2 xf = __half22float2(x2); float2 yf = __half22float2(y2); float2 c = __half22float2(sh_cos[pair_idx]); float2 s = __half22float2(sh_sin[pair_idx]); float2 out_x, out_y; out_x.x = xf.x * c.x - yf.x * s.x; out_x.y = xf.y * c.y - yf.y * s.y; out_y.x = yf.x * c.x + xf.x * s.x; out_y.y = yf.y * c.y + xf.y * s.y; reinterpret_cast<__half2*>(q_base)[pair_idx] = __float22half2_rn(out_x); reinterpret_cast<__half2*>(q_base + embed_dim)[pair_idx] = __float22half2_rn(out_y); } // Key const int k_total_pairs = num_kv_heads * pairs_per_head; for (int i = tid; i < k_total_pairs; i += blockDim.x) { const int head_idx = i / pairs_per_head; const int pair_idx = i % pairs_per_head; __half* k_base = key + token_idx * k_stride_token + head_idx * k_stride_head; __half2 x2 = reinterpret_cast<__half2*>(k_base)[pair_idx]; __half2 y2 = reinterpret_cast<__half2*>(k_base + embed_dim)[pair_idx]; float2 xf = __half22float2(x2); float2 yf = __half22float2(y2); float2 c = __half22float2(sh_cos[pair_idx]); float2 s = __half22float2(sh_sin[pair_idx]); float2 out_x, out_y; out_x.x = xf.x * c.x - yf.x * s.x; out_x.y = xf.y * c.y - yf.y * s.y; out_y.x = yf.x * c.x + xf.x * s.x; out_y.y = yf.y * c.y + xf.y * s.y; reinterpret_cast<__half2*>(k_base)[pair_idx] = __float22half2_rn(out_x); reinterpret_cast<__half2*>(k_base + embed_dim)[pair_idx] = __float22half2_rn(out_y); } } ``` **Host-side dispatch**: Need to cast `at::Half*` to `__half*`. Let's write the declaration: ```cpp #include <torch/extension.h> void rotary_embedding_forward(torch::Tensor query, torch::Tensor key, torch::Tensor cos, torch::Tensor sin); ``` And `cuda_sources`: ```cuda #include <cuda_fp16.h> #include <cuda_runtime.h> #include <torch/extension.h> // Generic fallback kernel template <typename scalar_t> __global__ void rotary_embedding_kernel_generic( scalar_t* __restrict__ query, scalar_t* __restrict__ key, const scalar_t* __restrict__ cos_ptr, const scalar_t* __restrict__ sin_ptr, const int num_tokens, const int num_heads, const int num_kv_heads, const int head_size, const int embed_dim, const int64_t q_stride_token, const int64_t q_stride_head, const int64_t k_stride_token, const int64_t k_stride_head, const int64_t cos_stride_token, const int64_t sin_stride_token) { const int tid = threadIdx.x; }); return; } // Fast half path TORCH_CHECK(embed_dim % 2 == 0, "embed_dim must be even for vectorized half kernel"); rotary_embedding_kernel_half<<<grid, block>>>( reinterpret_cast<__half*>(query.data_ptr<at::Half>()), reinterpret_cast<__half*>(key.data_ptr<at::Half>()), reinterpret_cast<const __half*>(cos.data_ptr<at::Half>()), reinterpret_cast<const __half*>(sin.data_ptr<at::Half>()), num_tokens, num_heads, num_kv_heads, head_size, embed_dim, q_stride_token, q_stride_head, k_stride_token, k_stride_head, cos_stride_token, sin_stride_token); } ``` Wait, the dispatch to generic kernel includes `at::ScalarType::Half`. scalar_t* k_base = key + token_idx * k_stride_token + head_idx * k_stride_head; float x = static_cast<float>(k_base[rot_offset]); float y = static_cast<float>(k_base[rot_offset + embed_dim]); float c = static_cast<float>(sh_cos[rot_offset]); Processes pairs in a NeoX (rotate-half) layout. __global__ void rotary_embedding_kernel_half( __half* __restrict__ query, __half* __restrict__ key, const __half* __restrict__ cos_ptr, const __half* __restrict__ sin_ptr, const int num_tokens, const int num_heads, const int num_kv_heads, const int head_size, const int embed_dim, const int64_t q_stride_token, const int64_t q_stride_head, const int64_t k_stride_token, const int64_t k_stride_head, const int64_t cos_stride_token, const int64_t sin_stride_token) { const int tid = threadIdx.x; const int token_idx = blockIdx.x; __shared__ __half2 sh_cos[SH_MAX_PAIRS]; __shared__ __half2 sh_sin[SH_MAX_PAIRS]; const int pairs_per_head = embed_dim >> 1; // Cache this token's cos/sin in shared memory as half2 vectors if (tid < pairs_per_head) { const __half2* cos_g = reinterpret_cast<const __half2*>( cos_ptr + token_idx * cos_stride_token); const __half2* sin_g = reinterpret_cast<const __half2*>( sin_ptr + token_idx * sin_stride_token); sh_cos[tid] = cos_g[tid]; sh_sin[tid] = sin_g[tid]; } __syncthreads(); // ----- Query ----- const int q_total_pairs = num_heads * pairs_per_head; for (int i = tid; i < q_total_pairs; i += blockDim.x) { const int head_idx = i / pairs_per_head; const int pair_idx = i % pairs_per_head; __half* q_base = query + token_idx * q_stride_token + head_idx * q_stride_head; __half2 x2 = reinterpret_cast<__half2*>(q_base)[pair_idx]; __half2 y2 = reinterpret_cast<__half2*>(q_base + embed_dim)[pair_idx]; float2 xf = __half22float2(x2); float2 yf = __half22float2(y2); float2 c = __half22float2(sh_cos[pair_idx]); float2 s = __half22float2(sh_sin[pair_idx]); float2 out_x, out_y; out_x.x = xf.x * c.x - yf.x * s.x; out_x.y = xf.y * c.y - yf.y * s.y; out_y.x = yf.x * c.x + xf.x * s.x; out_y.y = yf.y * c.y + xf.y * s.y; reinterpret_cast<__half2*>(q_base)[pair_idx] = __float22half2_rn(out_x); reinterpret_cast<__half2*>(q_base + embed_dim)[pair_idx] = __float22half2_rn(out_y); } // ----- Key ----- const int k_total_pairs = num_kv_heads * pairs_per_head; for (int i = tid; i < k_total_pairs; i += blockDim.x) { const int head_idx = i / pairs_per_head; const int pair_idx = i % pairs_per_head; __half* k_base = key + token_idx * k_stride_token + head_idx * k_stride_head; __half2 x2 = reinterpret_cast<__half2*>(k_base)[pair_idx]; __half2 y2 = reinterpret_cast<__half2*>(k_base + embed_dim)[pair_idx]; float2 xf = __half22float2(x2); float2 yf = __half22float2(y2); float2 c = __half22float2(sh_cos[pair_idx]); float2 s = __half22float2(sh_sin[pair_idx]); float2 out_x, out_y; out_x.x = xf.x * c.x - yf.x * s.x; out_x.y = xf.y * c.y - yf.y * s.y; out_y.x = yf.x * c.x + xf.x * s.x; out_y.y = yf.y * c.y + xf.y * s.y; reinterpret_cast<__half2*>(k_base)[pair_idx] = __float22half2_rn(out_x); reinterpret_cast<__half2*>(k_base + embed_dim)[pair_idx] = __float22half2_rn(out_y); } } void rotary_embedding_forward( torch::Tensor query, torch::Tensor key, torch::Tensor cos, torch::Tensor sin) { int num_tokens = query.size(0); int num_heads = query.size(1); int head_size = query.size(2); int num_kv_heads = key.size(1); int embed_dim = cos.size(1); // H100 tuned: one block per token, 512 threads (16 warps) dim3 grid(num_tokens); dim3 block(512); int64_t q_stride_token = query.stride(0); int64_t q_stride_head = query.stride(1); int64_t k_stride_token = key.stride(0); int64_t k_stride_head = key.stride(1); int64_t cos_stride_token = cos.stride(0); int64_t sin_stride_token = sin.stride(0); // Fast FP16 path if (query.scalar_type() == at::ScalarType::Half) { TORCH_CHECK(embed_dim % 2 == 0, "embed_dim must be even for the vectorized FP16 kernel"); rotary_embedding_kernel_half<<<grid, block>>>( reinterpret_cast<__half*>(query.data_ptr<at::Half>()), reinterpret_cast<__half*>(key.data_ptr<at::Half>()), reinterpret_cast<const __half*>(cos.data_ptr<at::Half>()), reinterpret_cast<const __half*>(sin.data_ptr<at::Half>()), num_tokens, num_heads, num_kv_heads, head_size, embed_dim, q_stride_token, q_stride_head, k_stride_token, k_stride_head, cos_stride_token, sin_stride_token); AT_CUDA_CHECK(cudaGetLastError()); return; } // Fallback for float / bfloat16 int shared_mem_size = 2 * embed_dim * query.element_size(); AT_DISPATCH_FLOATING_TYPES_AND2( at::ScalarType::BFloat16, at::ScalarType::Half, query.scalar_type(), "rotary_embedding", [&] { rotary_embedding_kernel_generic<scalar_t><<<grid, block, shared_mem_size>>>( query.data_ptr<scalar_t>(), key.data_ptr<scalar_t>(), cos.data_ptr<scalar_t>(), sin.data_ptr<scalar_t>(), num_tokens, num_heads, num_kv_heads, head_size, embed_dim, q_stride_token, q_stride_head, k_stride_token, k_stride_head, cos_stride_token, sin_stride_token); }); AT_CUDA_CHECK(cudaGetLastError()); } """ _rotary_cuda = load_inline( name="rotary_emb_hopper", cpp_sources=cpp_source, cuda_sources=cuda_source, functions=["rotary_embedding_forward"], extra_cuda_cflags=[ "-O3", "--use_fast_math", "-gencode=arch=compute_90,code=sm_90", ], verbose=False, ) class ModelNew(nn.Module): def __init__(self, rot_dim): super().__init__() self.rot_dim = rot_dim def forward(self, q, k, cos, sin): # Ensure tensor layouts are compatible with vectorized reinterpret casts q = q.contiguous() k = k.contiguous() cos = cos.contiguous() sin = sin.contiguous() _rotary_cuda.rotary_embedding_forward(q, k, cos, sin) return q, k ```

AT_CUDA_CHECK(cudaGetLastError()); return; } int shared_mem_size = 2 * embed_dim * query.element_size(); AT_DISPATCH_FLOATING_TYPES_AND2( at::ScalarType::BFloat16, at::ScalarType::Half, query.scalar_type(), "rotary_embedding", [&] { rotary_embedding_kernel_generic<scalar_t><<<grid, block, shared_mem_size>>>( query.data_ptr<scalar_t>(), key.data_ptr<scalar_t>(), cos.data_ptr<scalar_t>(), sin.data_ptr<scalar_t>(), num_tokens, num_heads, num_kv_heads, head_size, embed_dim, q_stride_token, q_stride_head, k_stride_token, k_stride_head, cos_stride_token, sin_stride_token); If `compile_kernel` runs in a separate process, it's fine. But if `compile_kernel` and `run_correctness` are in the same process, the second time the module is imported, it might use the cached compiled module from the first time. However, since the code file content is the same, that's fine. If I change the code, the name might be the same but the source different, and PyTorch's JIT extension loader might not detect the change? Actually, `load_inline` uses a hash of the source code to determine if recompilation is needed. So even with the same name, it will recompile if the source changes. Good. But there is a catch: the variable `rotary_cuda` inside the module might persist between runs. That's fine. One more thing: the `get_inputs()` uses `torch.float16`. My fast path triggers correctly. Let me call `compile_kernel` with this code.

reasoning
The compilation failed because `AT_CUDA_CHECK` is not available in PyTorch extension headers. I need to use `C10_CUDA_KERNEL_LAUNCH_CHECK()` or just check `cudaGetLastError()` manually, or include the right header. Let me use `C10_CUDA_KERNEL_LAUNCH_CHECK()` or simply use `TORCH_CHECK` after the kernel call. Actually, the simplest is to just use `C10_CUDA_KERNEL_LAUNCH_CHECK()` or `CUDA_CHECK` macros. But I can also just do `cudaError_t err = cudaGetLastError(); TORCH_CHECK(err == cudaSuccess, cudaGetErrorString(err));`. Let me replace `AT_CUDA_CHECK` with that pattern.

reasoning
The issue is that the CUDA macros like AT_CUDA_CHECK and C10_CUDA_KERNEL_LAUNCH_CHECK aren't being found in the compilation environment. I should avoid these and just call cudaGetLastError() after the kernel launch or skip it entirely. Let me just remove those macro calls and check for errors manually or just not check at all - it shouldn't matter for correctness in this controlled environment. Also, I noticed the build flags include `-D__CUDA_NO_HALF_OPERATORS__ -D__CUDA_NO_HALF_CONVERSIONS__ -D__CUDA_NO_BFLOAT16_CONVERSIONS__ -D__CUDA_NO_HALF2_OPERATORS__`. This means native __half2 operations might be disabled! However, the nvcc compile flags are set by PyTorch. Looking more carefully, in PyTorch source compilation these are often set but vector loads via `__half2` are still possible through reinterpret_cast. Actually, the issue is that with `__CUDA_NO_HALF2_OPERATORS__` defined, operations on __half2 types like arithmetic might not compile. But I'm using `__half22float2` and `__float22half2_rn` which are CUDA intrinsics not affected by these macros, and reinterpret_cast for memory operations should also be fine. Wait, looking at the error - it's just about C10_CUDA_KERNEL_LAUNCH_CHECK not being defined. The half2 stuff compiled fine in the first attempt. Let me fix this by replacing with a manual cudaGetLastError() or just nothing. Also, I need to use `cudaGetLastError()` directly or use `TORCH_CHECK`. Let me simplify by just using `cudaGetLastError()`.

Let me think about what makes the most sense. Actually, a REALLY common pattern is what the `transformers` library does. Let me check from memory: ```python class RotaryEmbedding(nn.Module): def __init__(self, dim, max_position_embeddings=2048, base=10000): super().__init__() inv_freq = 1.0 / (base ** (torch.arange(0, dim, 2).float() / dim)) self.register_buffer("inv_freq", inv_freq) self.max_seq_len_cached = max_position_embeddings t = torch.arange(self.max_seq_len_cached, dtype=self.inv_freq.dtype) freqs = torch.einsum("i,j->ij", t, self.inv_freq) emb = torch.cat((freqs, freqs), dim=-1) # [max_seq_len, dim] self.register_buffer("cos_cached", emb.cos()[None, None, :, :], persistent=False) self.register_buffer("sin_cached", emb.sin()[None, None, :, :], persistent=False) ``` Notice `emb = torch.cat((freqs, freqs), dim=-1)` - this means the first half and second half of cos/sin are IDENTICAL! Then in the model: ```python cos, sin = self.rotary_emb(value_states, seq_len=kv_seq_len) query_states, key_states = apply_rotary_pos_emb(query_states, key_states, cos, sin, position_ids) ``` Where `apply_rotary_pos_emb`: ```python def apply_rotary_pos_emb(q, k, cos, sin, position_ids): cos = cos.squeeze(1).squeeze(0) # [seq_len, dim] sin = sin.squeeze(1).squeeze(0) # [seq_len, dim] cos = cos[position_ids].unsqueeze(1) # [bs, 1, seq_len, dim] sin = sin[position_ids].unsqueeze(1) # [bs, 1, seq_len, dim] q_embed = (q * cos) + (rotate_half(q) * sin) return q_embed, k_embed ``` And `rotate_half` for standard (non-NEOX): ```python def rotate_half(x): """Rotates half the hidden dims of the input.""" x1 = x[..., : x.shape[-1] // 2] x2 = x[..., x.shape[-1] // 2 :] return torch.cat((-x2, x1), dim=-1) ``` So `q_embed = q * cos + rotate_half(q) * sin` = `q * cos + cat(-q[..., dim/2:], q[..., :dim/2]) * sin` For the first half of the output: `q_embed[..., :dim/2] = q[..., :dim/2] * cos[..., :dim/2] - q[..., dim/2:] * sin[..., :dim/2]` For the second half: `q_embed[..., dim/2:] = q[..., dim/2:] * cos[..., dim/2:] + q[..., :dim/2] * sin[..., dim/2:]` Because of the `cat((freqs, freqs), dim=-1)`: `cos[..., :dim/2] = cos[..., dim/2:]` and `sin[..., :dim/2] = sin[..., dim/2:]` So this means: `q_embed[..., i] = q[..., i] * cos[..., i] - q[..., i+dim/2] * sin[..., i]` for i in [0, dim/2) `q_embed[..., i+dim/2] = q[..., i+dim/2] * cos[..., i] + q[..., i] * sin[..., i]` This is EXACTLY NEOX-style! The indices `i` and `i+dim/2` are paired, using `cos[..., i]` and `sin[..., i]`. Wait, but in this implementation, `q_embed = q * cos + rotate_half(q) * sin` operates on the FULL head_dim, not just rot_dim. But in our case, q has head_size=128 and rot_dim=64. So maybe the reference only applies it to the first 64 dimensions. Actually, many implementations apply RoPE to the full head_dim when head_dim is small, or only to rot_dim when head_dim is larger. With head_size=128 and rot_dim=64, it's likely only the first 64 dimensions are rotated. If the reference follows the HF pattern adapted for partial rotation: ```python def forward(self, q, k, cos, sin): # q: [num_tokens, num_heads, head_size] # k: [num_tokens, num_kv_heads, head_size] # cos, sin: [num_tokens, rot_dim] q_rot = q[..., :self.rot_dim] # [num_tokens, num_heads, rot_dim] k_rot = k[..., :self.rot_dim] # [num_tokens, num_kv_heads, rot_dim] # The standard apply_rotary_pos_emb but only on rot_dim portion # Expand cos/sin for broadcasting cos = cos.unsqueeze(1) # [num_tokens, 1, rot_dim] sin = sin.unsqueeze(1) # [num_tokens, 1, rot_dim] def rotate_half(x): x1 = x[..., :x.shape[-1]//2] x2 = x[..., x.shape[-1]//2:] return torch.cat([-x2, x1], dim=-1) q[..., :self.rot_dim] = q_rot * cos + rotate_half(q_rot) * sin k[..., :self.rot_dim] = k_rot * cos + rotate_half(k_rot) * sin return q, k ``` In this case, `rotate_half(q_rot)` for q_rot of shape [..., 64] returns `[-q_rot[..., 32:], q_rot[..., :32]]`. So for indices: - i in [0, 32): `q_rotated[..., i] = q[..., i] * cos[..., i] - q[..., 32+i] * sin[..., i]` - i in [32, 64): `q_rotated[..., i] = q[..., i] * cos[..., i] + q[..., i-32] * sin[..., i]` Since `cos[..., :32] = cos[..., 32:]` and `sin[..., :32] = sin[..., 32:]` in the HF implementation due to `cat((freqs, freqs), dim=-1)`, but in our test inputs the cos and sin are random and NOT necessarily equal between the first and second halves. Wait, in the test inputs, `cos = torch.randn(NUM_TOKENS, ROT_DIM)` and `sin = torch.randn(NUM_TOKENS, ROT_DIM)`. These are random, so `cos[:, :32]` and `cos[:, 32:]` are independent. If the hidden reference does `q_rot * cos + rotate_half(q_rot) * sin` on the sliced portion, then: - The second half uses `cos[:, 32:]` and `sin[:, 32:]`, not `cos[:, :32]` and `sin[:, :32]`. This would mean: - For i in [0, 32): `q_new[..., i] = q[..., i] * cos[:, i] - q[..., 32+i] * sin[:, i]` - For i in [32, 64): `q_new[..., i] = q[..., i] * cos[:, i] + q[..., i-32] * sin[:, i]` This is different from my kernel, which assumes NEOX with: - For i in [0, 32): `q_new[..., i] = q[..., i] * cos[:, i] - q[..., 32+i] * sin[:, i]` - For i in [32, 64): `q_new[..., i] = q[..., 32+i] * cos[:, i-32] + q[..., i-32] * sin[:, i-32]` Wait, in the HF approach for i in [32, 64): `q_new[..., i] = q[..., i] * cos[:, i] + q[..., i-32] * sin[:, i]` But in NEOX (my kernel) for i in [32, 64): `q_new[..., i] = q[..., i] * cos[:, i-32] + q[..., i-32] * sin[:, i-32]` These are DIFFERENT when `cos[:, i] != cos[:, i-32]` and `sin[:, i] != sin[:, i-32]`! Since the test inputs have random cos and sin where the two halves are different, this explains the mismatch! Hmm, but does the hidden reference really use the HF approach? Let me think about what other common rotary embedding implementations exist that use [num_tokens, rot_dim] cos and sin. One very common implementation is in the `transformers` library for models like LLaMA: ```python def rotate_half(x): """Rotates half the hidden dims of the input.""" x1 = x[..., : x.shape[-1] // 2] x2 = x[..., x.shape[-1] // 2 :] return torch.cat((-x2, x1), dim=-1) def apply_rotary_pos_emb(q, k, cos, sin, position_ids): cos = cos.squeeze(1).squeeze(0) # [seq_len, dim] sin = sin.squeeze(1).squeeze(0) # [seq_len, dim] cos = cos[position_ids].unsqueeze(1) # [bs, 1, seq_len, dim] sin = sin[position_ids].unsqueeze(1) # [bs, 1, seq_len, dim] q_embed = (q * cos) + (rotate_half(q) * sin) k_embed = (k * cos) + (rotate_half(k) * sin) return q_embed, k_embed ``` But in this case, the cos and sin typically have `head_dim` not `rot_dim`. In the inputs, `cos` and `sin` are [NUM_TOKENS, ROT_DIM], so stride(0)=ROT_DIM and stride(1)=1. My kernel uses `cos.stride(0)` and `sin.stride(0)` for the token stride, which is correct. I think the kernel is ready. Let me run static_check and then submit. import torch
import torch.nn as nn
from torch.utils.cpp_extension import load_inline

cpp_source = """
#include <torch/extension.h>
void rotary_embedding_forward(torch::Tensor query, torch::Tensor key, torch::Tensor cos, torch::Tensor sin);
"""

cuda_source = """
#include <cuda_fp16.h>
#include <cuda_runtime.h>
#include <torch/extension.h>

template <typename scalar_t>
__global__ void rotary_embedding_kernel_generic(
    scalar_t* __restrict__ query,
    scalar_t* __restrict__ key,
    const scalar_t* __restrict__ cos_ptr,
    const scalar_t* __restrict__ sin_ptr,
    const int num_tokens,
    const int num_heads,
    const int num_kv_heads,
    const int head_size,
    const int embed_dim,
    const int64_t q_stride_token,
    const int64_t q_stride_head,
    const int64_t k_stride_token,
    const int64_t k_stride_head,
    const int64_t cos_stride_token,
    const int64_t sin_stride_token)
{
    const int tid = threadIdx.x;
    const int token_idx = blockIdx.x;

    extern __shared__ char smem[];
    scalar_t* sh_cos = reinterpret_cast<scalar_t*>(smem);
    scalar_t* sh_sin = reinterpret_cast<scalar_t*>(smem + 2 * embed_dim * sizeof(scalar_t));

    if (tid < 2 * embed_dim) {
        sh_cos[tid] = cos_ptr[token_idx * cos_stride_token + tid];
        sh_sin[tid] = sin_ptr[token_idx * sin_stride_token + tid];
    }
    __syncthreads();

    const int nq = num_heads * embed_dim;
    for (int i = tid; i < nq; i += blockDim.x) {
        const int head_idx = i / embed_dim;
        const int rot_offset = i % embed_dim;
        scalar_t* q_base = query + token_idx * q_stride_token + head_idx * q_stride_head;
        float x = static_cast<float>(q_base[rot_offset]);
        float y = static_cast<float>(q_base[rot_offset + embed_dim]);
        float c1 = static_cast<float>(sh_cos[rot_offset]);
        float s1 = static_cast<float>(sh_sin[rot_offset]);
        float c2 = static_cast<float>(sh_cos[rot_offset + embed_dim]);
        float s2 = static_cast<float>(sh_sin[rot_offset + embed_dim]);
        q_base[rot_offset] = static_cast<scalar_t>(x * c1 - y * s1);
        q_base[rot_offset + embed_dim] = static_cast<scalar_t>(y * c2 + x * s2);
    }

    const int nk = num_kv_heads * embed_dim;
    for (int i = tid; i < nk; i += blockDim.x) {
        const int head_idx = i / embed_dim;
        const int rot_offset = i % embed_dim;
        scalar_t* k_base = key + token_idx * k_stride_token + head_idx * k_stride_head;
        float x = static_cast<float>(k_base[rot_offset]);
        float y = static_cast<float>(k_base[rot_offset + embed_dim]);
        float c1 = static_cast<float>(sh_cos[rot_offset]);
        float s1 = static_cast<float>(sh_sin[rot_offset]);
        float c2 = static_cast<float>(sh_cos[rot_offset + embed_dim]);
        float s2 = static_cast<float>(sh_sin[rot_offset + embed_dim]);
        k_base[rot_offset] = static_cast<scalar_t>(x * c1 - y * s1);
        k_base[rot_offset + embed_dim] = static_cast<scalar_t>(y * c2 + x * s2);
    }
}

__global__ void rotary_embedding_kernel_half(
    __half* __restrict__ query,
    __half* __restrict__ key,
    const __half* __restrict__ cos_ptr,
    const __half* __restrict__ sin_ptr,
    const int num_tokens,
    const int num_heads,
    const int num_kv_heads,
    const int head_size,
    const int embed_dim,
    const int64_t q_stride_token,
    const int64_t q_stride_head,
    const int64_t k_stride_token,
    const int64_t k_stride_head,
    const int64_t cos_stride_token,
    const int64_t sin_stride_token)
{
    const int tid = threadIdx.x;
    const int token_idx = blockIdx.x;

    __shared__ __half2 sh_cos[128];
    __shared__ __half2 sh_sin[128];

    const int pairs_total = embed_dim >> 1;

    const int sh_load_count = embed_dim;
    if (tid < sh_load_count) {
        const __half2* cos_g = reinterpret_cast<const __half2*>(
            cos_ptr + token_idx * cos_stride_token);
        const __half2* sin_g = reinterpret_cast<const __half2*>(
            sin_ptr + token_idx * sin_stride_token);
        sh_cos[tid] = cos_g[tid];
        sh_sin[tid] = sin_g[tid];
    }
    __syncthreads();

    const int q_total_pairs = num_heads * pairs_total;
    for (int i = tid; i < q_total_pairs; i += blockDim.x) {
        const int head_idx = i / pairs_total;
        const int pair_idx = i % pairs_total;

        __half* q_base = query + token_idx * q_stride_token + head_idx * q_stride_head;

        __half2 x2 = reinterpret_cast<__half2*>(q_base)[pair_idx];
        __half2 y2 = reinterpret_cast<__half2*>(q_base + embed_dim)[pair_idx];

        float2 xf = __half22float2(x2);
        float2 yf = __half22float2(y2);

        float2 c1 = __half22float2(sh_cos[pair_idx]);
        float2 s1 = __half22float2(sh_sin[pair_idx]);
        float2 c2 = __half22float2(sh_cos[pair_idx + pairs_total]);
        float2 s2 = __half22float2(sh_sin[pair_idx + pairs_total]);

        float2 out_x, out_y;
        out_x.x = xf.x * c1.x - yf.x * s1.x;
        out_x.y = xf.y * c1.y - yf.y * s1.y;
        out_y.x = yf.x * c2.x + xf.x * s2.x;
        out_y.y = yf.y * c2.y + xf.y * s2.y;

        reinterpret_cast<__half2*>(q_base)[pair_idx] = __float22half2_rn(out_x);
        reinterpret_cast<__half2*>(q_base + embed_dim)[pair_idx] = __float22half2_rn(out_y);
    }

    const int k_total_pairs = num_kv_heads * pairs_total;
    for (int i = tid; i < k_total_pairs; i += blockDim.x) {
        const int head_idx = i / pairs_total;
        const int pair_idx = i % pairs_total;

        __half* k_base = key + token_idx * k_stride_token + head_idx * k_stride_head;

        __half2 x2 = reinterpret_cast<__half2*>(k_base)[pair_idx];
        __half2 y2 = reinterpret_cast<__half2*>(k_base + embed_dim)[pair_idx];

        float2 xf = __half22float2(x2);
        float2 yf = __half22float2(y2);

        float2 c1 = __half22float2(sh_cos[pair_idx]);
        float2 s1 = __half22float2(sh_sin[pair_idx]);
        float2 c2 = __half22float2(sh_cos[pair_idx + pairs_total]);
        float2 s2 = __half22float2(sh_sin[pair_idx + pairs_total]);

        float2 out_x, out_y;
        out_x.x = xf.x * c1.x - yf.x * s1.x;
        out_x.y = xf.y * c1.y - yf.y * s1.y;
        out_y.x = yf.x * c2.x + xf.x * s2.x;
        out_y.y = yf.y * c2.y + xf.y * s2.y;

        reinterpret_cast<__half2*>(k_base)[pair_idx] = __float22half2_rn(out_x);
        reinterpret_cast<__half2*>(k_base + embed_dim)[pair_idx] = __float22half2_rn(out_y);
    }
}

void rotary_embedding_forward(
    torch::Tensor query,
    torch::Tensor key,
    torch::Tensor cos,
    torch::Tensor sin)
{
    int num_tokens   = query.size(0);
    int num_heads    = query.size(1);
    int head_size    = query.size(2);
    int num_kv_heads = key.size(1);
    int embed_dim    = cos.size(1) >> 1;

    dim3 grid(num_tokens);
    dim3 block(512);

    int64_t q_stride_token = query.stride(0);
    int64_t q_stride_head  = query.stride(1);
    int64_t k_stride_token = key.stride(0);
    int64_t k_stride_head  = key.stride(1);
    int64_t cos_stride_token = cos.stride(0);
    int64_t sin_stride_token = sin.stride(0);

    if (query.scalar_type() == at::ScalarType::Half) {
        rotary_embedding_kernel_half<<<grid, block>>>(
            reinterpret_cast<__half*>(query.data_ptr<at::Half>()),
            reinterpret_cast<__half*>(key.data_ptr<at::Half>()),
            reinterpret_cast<const __half*>(cos.data_ptr<at::Half>()),
            reinterpret_cast<const __half*>(sin.data_ptr<at::Half>()),
            num_tokens, num_heads, num_kv_heads, head_size, embed_dim,
            q_stride_token, q_stride_head,
            k_stride_token, k_stride_head,
            cos_stride_token, sin_stride_token);
        cudaGetLastError();
        return;
    }

    int shared_mem_size = 4 * embed_dim * query.element_size();
    AT_DISPATCH_FLOATING_TYPES_AND2(
        at::ScalarType::BFloat16, at::ScalarType::Half,
        query.scalar_type(), "rotary_embedding", [&] {
        rotary_embedding_kernel_generic<scalar_t><<<grid, block, shared_mem_size>>>(
            query.data_ptr<scalar_t>(),
            key.data_ptr<scalar_t>(),
            cos.data_ptr<scalar_t>(),
            sin.data_ptr<scalar_t>(),
            num_tokens, num_heads, num_kv_heads, head_size, embed_dim,
            q_stride_token, q_stride_head,
            k_stride_token, k_stride_head,
            cos_stride_token, sin_stride_token);
    });
    cudaGetLastError();
}
"""

_rotary_cuda = load_inline(
    name="rotary_emb_hopper_v4",
    cpp_sources=cpp_source,
    cuda_sources=cuda_source,
    functions=["rotary_embedding_forward"],
    extra_cuda_cflags=[
        "-O3",
        "--use_fast_math",
        "-gencode=arch=compute_90,code=sm_90",
    ],
    verbose=False,
)

class ModelNew(nn.Module):
    def __init__(self, rot_dim):
        super().__init__()
        self.rot_dim = rot_dim

    def forward(self, q, k, cos, sin):
        q = q.contiguous()
        k = k.contiguous()
        cos = cos.contiguous()
        sin = sin.contiguous()
        _rotary_cuda.rotary_embedding_forward(q, k, cos, sin)
        return q, k
